 Preliminary Program
2026 SPSA Annual Conference

   New Orleans, Louisiana
    January 14-17, 2026

            v.7.0
       Welcome, From SPSA President Elect
               Kirk A. Randazzo
Welcome to the annual meeting of the Southern Political Science
Association, where research and scholarship meet southern
hospitality. Our conference serves multiple purposes: advancing
research on political challenges across numerous topics and
disciplines, fostering professional connections among participants,
and developing standards of excellence among scholars and
practitioners.

The Southern Political Science Association is an organization with
strong values of responsibility and integrity. We pride ourselves on
combining rigorous academic discourse with warm southern
hospitality. Our nightly receptions offer valuable opportunities to
network with colleagues, share ideas, and build lasting professional
relationships.

We hope your experience here is both intellectually stimulating
and personally enjoyable. Please take some time to explore New
Orleans, attend panel sessions, and make the most of your stay.
Enjoy Southern’s Hospitality!

Kirk A. Randazzo
SPSA President Elect

     2100            CwC #8: Political and Human Behavior in Crises
        Thursday     Conference Within A Conference
  8:00am-9:15am
Churchill B2 - 2nd   Chair
            Floor        Ashley Ross, Texas A&M University at Galveston
                     Participants
                     When the Tsunami Comes at Night: Environmental and Social Predictors of Evacuation Behavior
                          Tristan Wu, University of North Texas
                          Alex Greer, University at Albany
                          Yuki Li, Skidmore College
                          Hideyuki Shiroshita, Kansai University
                     No relief: Why do aid - dependent states fail to seek humanitarian aid?
                          Allison Grossman, Tulane University
                     Windfall or Washout? Disaster Relief, Corruption, & Candidate Quality in Local Governments
                          Gavin Kiger, Emory University
                     A Model of Culpability and Politician's Crisis Response: Over - reaction and Under - reaction
                          Aaron Du, Washington University in St. Louis
                     Storms and Ballots: The Electoral Consequences of Tropical Cyclones in Malawi
                          Jack Fernandes, Ohio State University
                     Discussant
                          Courtney Page-Tan, Indiana University Indianapolis
                     This panel explores how political actors and the public make calculated decisions during disasters — not
                     necessarily to optimize relief or resilience. These papers demonstrate how political actors often seek to
                     manage perceptions, avoid blame, or gain political advantage during and after disaster while members of
                     the public take cues from these political reactions.

   2100           Regulation and the Environment: Gaining New Imperatives and Losing Old Perspectives
     Thursday     Environmental Politics and Policy
8:00am-9:15am
  Norwich - 3rd   Participants
         Floor    Bridging Political Climate Concern and Experience: Understanding Divergent Pathways to Climate Policy
                       Support
                       Yoonyoung Cho, Georgia State University
                  Power and Politics in Climate Adaptation: Authority, Knowledge, and Resource Inequality in Nepal’s
                       Multi-level Governance
                       Suman Acharya, School of Politics, Security, and International Affairs (SPSIA), University of
                       Central Florida
                  Screening for Justice
                       David Konisky, SPEA Indiana University
                       Christopher Michael Reenock, Florida State University
                       James Stewart, Florida State University
                  Stakeholder Dynamics and Low Carbon Technologies in Japan
                       Pinar Temocin, University of Tokyo
                  From Peace to Policy: How Resource Provisions in Peace Agreements Drive Climate Legislation in Post-
                       Conflict Societies
                       Elisa D'Amico, University College Dublin
                       Tahlia Mullen, World Bank
                  Democracy and Environmental Performance: A Cross-National Study
                       Juheon Lee, Midwestern State University
                  The nature of environmental and energy regulation is changing around the world and within the United
                  States. Changes to regulation involve a transformation in imperatives and process under regulatory policy,
                  including changes with respect to how contracts (and grievances) are settled under regulation. These
                  papers address ongoing changes to regulation and the existing contract between regulators, regulated
                  entities, and the public.

     2300            CwC #7: Political Economy of Autocracy

                                                        2500
     2500            CwC #8: Panel Discussion: Introducing the United States Emergency & Disaster Management
                     Congress (USEDMC
        Thursday     Conference Within A Conference
  2:00pm-3:15pm
Churchill B2 - 2nd   Discussants
            Floor         Tanya Buhler Corbin, Embry-Riddle Aeronautical University
                          Arthur Simental, US Emergency & Disaster Management Congress
                     Disaster management and federal disaster policies are under scrutiny and debate in the U.S., with major
                     institutions and agencies questioning and re-envisioning their structures, roles, and functions. These
                     profound changes in the US emergency management s y stem are occurring at an accelerated pace, with
                     input from the academic and practitioner community largely absent. Instead, disaster and emergency
                     management policies are being developed and enacted based on partisan interests rather than current
                     science and academic research. As disaster policy scholars have established, advocacy groups are
                     necessary for successful policy change, which is lacking in th e disaster policy domain. An effort is now
                     underway to bring together the disaster management community of scholars and practitioners through the
                     United States Emergency & Disaster Management Congress (USEDMC), a nonpartisan, nonprofit
                     professional association dedicated to influencing and shaping policy through legislative advocacy,
                     education, and through the establishment of a unified national emergency & disaster management
                     professional standard known as the “model emergency management law” and shared governance. Newly
                     established, USEDMC represents emergency management professionals across the United State s from all
                     U.S. states, territories, and tribes. In this roundtable discussion, we offer an overview of the organization,
                     priorities, and progress of the organization and engage academic and practitioner stakeholders in a
                     discussion of how to successfully ensure a robust, effective, and sustainable emergency and disaster
                     management advocacy organization in the future.

   2500           The Politics of Culture, Communication and Finitude in Nietzsche, Jaspers and Arendt
     Thursday     Political Theory
2:00pm-3:15pm
 Magazine - 3rd   Chair
         Floor        Constantine Vassiliou, University of Colorado, Colorado Springs
                  Participants
                  Hannah Arendt and the Co-Verification of Reality through Action
                       Natalie Moreira, Baylor University
                  Nietzsche as Culture Physician: Nietzsche on “Philosophy in the Tragic Age of the Greeks” and his self-
                       understanding as “the last disciple of the philosopher Dionysus”
                       Dwight Allman, Baylor University
                  Karl Jaspers and the Politics of Finitude
                       Lee Ward, Baylor University
                  Beyond the Ascetic Cocoon: An Examination of the Science of Politics in Nietzsche’s Genealogy of
                       Morals
                       Paul Kirkland, Carthage College
                  Discussant
                       Boleslaw Kabala, Tarleton State University
                  Twentieth-century German political philosophy was profoundly influenced by Friedrich Nietzsche’s
                  recovery of culture as a tool for diagnosing what he took to be the malaise of modern liberal democratic
                  life. This panel explores Nietzsche’s cultural politics and the echoes of a cultural approach to the study of
                  politics in Hannah Arendt’s theory of communication and action, as well as Karl Jaspers’ account of a
                  politics based on finitude. Dwight Allman reconsiders the impact of Nietzsche’s early work The Birth of
                  Tragedy by examining his early notes and unpublished writings (the Nachlass) and finds the roots of
                  Nietzsche’s adoption of ancient Greek tragic culture, which would prove pivotal in his embrace of the
                  persona “the last disciple of the philosopher Dionysius” in his later works Beyond Good and Evil and
                  Twilight of the Idols. Paul Kirkland explores Nietzsche's argument about the cultural effects of the ascetic
                  ideal which he associates with the modern scientific project. Kirkland argues that in Genealogy of Morals
                  Nietzsche sought to fashion a new appearance for philosophy that does not require the mask of asceticism.
                  Natalie Moreira engages the question of the philosophic meaning of culture by highlighting Arendt’s
                  efforts to frame a political epistemology that can ground the multiple perspectives required to situate
                  oneself in the realm of human affairs. For Moreira, one of Arendt’s signal achievements was to articulate
                  a theory of communication that contributes to the improvised actions reflected in the individual’s right to
                  organize a creative participatory political community. Lee Ward turns to Arendt’s mentor Karl Jaspers.
                  Ward argues that this doyen of abstract existentialist philosophers actually had a very moderate political
                  teaching grounded on his concept of “boundary situations” [Grenzsituationen]. For Jaspers the individual
                  human being can only become an authentic self through a series of “leaps” from self to Existenz framed
                  by the limits placed on action by material, social and political conditions rooted in historical reality. The
                  paper concludes that Jaspers’ politics of finitude offers a compelling response to the integralist political
                  and theological ambitions of many contemporary “post-liberal” thinkers such as Patrick Deneen and
                  Adrian Vermeule. Taken altogether, the papers in this panel each highlight aspects of the relation of
                  culture and politics that continue to resonate in contemporary life.
                                                          2500
       2500            The South and the Architecture of Democracy
          Thursday     American Political Development
   2:00pm-3:15pm
 Parish - 3rd Floor    Chair
                           Jeff Selinger, Bowdoin College
                       Participants
                       Southern Public Architecture and the Emergence of the American Capitol Type
                            Keith Gaddie, Texas Christian University
                       The Creation of the Texas Courthouse Type
                            James Riddlesperger, Texas Christian University
                       Contentious Spaces and Memorialization of the Confederacy
                            Scott Huffmon, Winthrop University
                       Context and Culture in Southern Mural Art
                            Jocelyn Evans, University of West Florida
                       This panel presents scholarship on the development of modern American public architecture in the context
                       of the American South. Papers engage topics including the relationship of the settlement frontier to the
                       design of courthouses and public spaces; the emergence of the American capitol type from Southern
                       architecture; the evolution and meaning of southern public art; and Confederate monuments and
                       memorializations.

     2600            Author Meets Critics-The Politics of Individualism
        Thursday     Public Opinion
 3:30pm-4:45pm
Canal - 3rd Floor    Chairs
                         Kelly D. Patterson, Brigham Young University
                         Chris Karpowitz, Brigham Young University
                     Discussants
                          Julie Wronski, University of Mississippi
                          Bethany Albertson, University of Texas at Austin
                          Lee Ward, Baylor University
                          Julia Azari, Marquette University
                     Individualism is a quintessential American value, manifesting in emphases on hard work and economic
                     independence. But there is another key aspect to individualism: moral autonomy, which must be
                     understood in the context of an individual's relationship to authority. Autonomy has been a central focus
                     for political theory but overlooked in the empirical measures most often used by political scientists The
                     Politics of Individualism (2025, Oxford University Press) emphasizes the importance of moral autonomy
                     by examining the relationship between autonomy, the self, and institutions. The book explores the
                     conceptual dimensions of individualism, introduces a new empirical measure ("moral individualism"), and
                     evaluates its relationship to civic life. People who score highly on the moral individualism scale are less
                     likely to commit to public goods and to engage in collective efforts to alleviate common problems, even
                     in the midst of a pandemic that threatened the physical health of millions around the world. In light of
                     these findings, the book assesses the danger that moral individualism may pose to the maintenance of
                     democratic institutions and the common goals and beliefs that help bind a nation together. In this panel,
                     our aim is to explore the empirical advances made in The Politics of Individualism and their implications.
                     Because a central goal of the panel is to bring together both political theorists and empirical political
                     scientists, we anticipate the panel will include both types of scholars. We are still in the process of
                     finalizing the list of critics, but our aim for the panel will be to provide a brief overview of the argument
                     in the book, followed by constructive discussion from each of four critics. Given the content of the book,
                     we view this panel as a unique opportunity to promote dialogue between political philosophers and
                     empirical researchers as they explore the book and its findings.

                                                       2700
     2700            Thucydides and Plato: Thinking Through the Cave of Empire
       Thursday      Political Theory
  5:00pm-6:15pm
   Magazine - 3rd    Chair
           Floor         Borden Flanagan, American University
                     Participants
                     What’s So Good About the Good? Pericles and Socrates on the Politics of Eros
                          Borden Flanagan, American University
                     A Metaphysical Thucydides? Reading the ‘History’ in Light of Plato’s Timaeus and Critias
                          Bernard J Dobski, Assumption University
                     Thucydidean Dialogue and Plato
                          Charlotte Thomas, Mercer University
                     Discussant
                          Clifford Orwin, University of Toronto
                     Many commentators have written on the Peloponnesian war as it figures as the historical setting of
                     Platonic dialogues, but relatively little attention has been paid to Plato’s relation to Thucydides the thinker
                     or Thucydides the writer. The depth of Thucydides’ account of politics and “the human thing” and the
                     artistry of his text are widely accepted, so the neglect is strange. The Platonic dialogues offer an account
                     of Socrates, who brought philosophy down to the city, and Thucydides, Socrates’ contemporary, is
                     arguably the greatest and certainly the most immediate of Plato’s predecessors. Once the question of their
                     connection is raised signs of Thucydides’ influence on Plato emerge, as do more surprising indications:
                     reading Plato in light of Thucydides allows one to read Thucydides in light of Plato. That is, alertness to
                     Plato’s artistry and to his account of how philosophic questions emerge from political ones allows for a
                     more philosophically rich understanding of the substance and procedure of Thucydides’ text. This panel
                     brings together scholars of Thucydides and Plato to begin to give the connection between the two thinkers
                     it’s due.

     3100           CwC #5: Measurement Challenges
         Friday     Conference Within A Conference
 8:00am-9:15am
Camp - 3rd Floor    Chair
                        Diana Owen, Georgetown University
                    Participants
                    Empowering Tomorrow's Voters: Does Community - Based Curriculum Cultivate Civic Engagement
                         Among High School Students?
                         Sarah Reckhow, Michigan State University
                    Broadening our Concepts of Civic Outcomes: Validating a Youth Civic Engagement Scale
                         Kelly Siegel-Stechler, Interactivity Foundation
                    Studying School Board Policymaking at Scale by Extracting Image, Voice, and Text Data from Local
                         Public Meetings
                         Tyler Simko, University of Michigan
                         Mirya Holman, University of Houston
                    Civic Education and Youth Political Engagement in Rural America
                         Eleni Mantas-Kourounis, Sacred Heart University
                    Discussants
                         Christopher Chapp, St. Olaf College
                         Kate Destler, Western Washington University
                    “ Civics ” encompasses so many different concepts, from content knowledge about the branches of
                    government to actual participation in political institutions , that the field needs to make strides in defining
                    and measuring the phenomena we hope to understand. This panel includes papers that address these
                    challenges from different approaches. First, Sarah Reckhow compares curricula in terms of the
                    empowerment they provide to students. Then, Kelly Siegel - Stechler evaluates a scale measuring students
                    ’ civic engagement. Tyler Simko utilizes state - of - the - art software to obtain measures of civic
                    participation by gender at school board meetings. Finally, Eleni Mantas - Kourounis assesses the civic
                    opportunities that exist in rural communities in the United States.

                  moved from general findings to context-conditional results that distinguish disaster-recovery impacts of
                  different institutions, attitudes, and systems. This grouping of papers offers a glimpse into new research
                  that explores context-influence in disaster recovery and policymaking.

     3200           CwC #5: Attitudes toward Civic Education
         Friday     Conference Within A Conference
9:30am-10:45am
Camp - 3rd Floor    Chair
                        Eleni Mantas-Kourounis, Sacred Heart University
                    Participants
                    Teachers ’ Perspectives on Social and Emotional Learning in the Civics Classroom
                         Diana Owen, Georgetown University
                    Preliminary Insights into Perceptions of Teaching Indigenous History to Elementary School Students
                         Sarah Burnham, CIRCLE at Tufts University
                    Do the Behavioral Effects of Critical Civic Education Content Persist Over Time? Evidence from a Panel
                         Study
                         Matt Nelsen, University of Miami
                    Division, Unity, and Teaching Foundational Documents: A Brief Survey of Public Opinion
                         Jason Giersch, UNC Charlotte
                    Discussants
                         Logan Dancey, Wesleyan University
                         Isobel Heck, University of Rochester
                    Despite the widespread support to increasing civic education in the United States, exactly what that
                    education should include may not draw as much agreement. First, Diana Owen assesses teachers ’
                    attitudes toward the role social and emotional learning can play in classrooms studying civic education.
                    Then, Sarah Burnham finds that elementary teachers vary in their comfort with teaching indigenous
                    histories . Next, Matt Nelson tests whether critical civic education – in the form of short videos shown to a
                    national sample of adults – has las ting impacts on willingness to donate to social causes. Finally, Jason
                    Giersch finds that framing civic education as either divisive or uniting has no effect on respondents ’
                    opinions, but their political and racial identities do.

      3400            Political Behavior in Latin America
           Friday     Comparative Political Behavior and Electoral Systems
 12:30pm-1:45pm
 Ascot - 3rd Floor    Chair
                          Isabel Laterzo-Tingley, University of Texas at Austin
                      Participants
                      Beyond Partisanship: Theory and Methods
                           Nicolás de la Cerda, Tulane University
                      Bringing Communities Back to Community Policing Why Robust Citizen Participation Deepens Support
                           for Community Police in Indigenous Mexico
                           Natán Skigin, University of Georgia
                           Guillermo Trejo, University of Notre Dame
                           Shannan Mattiace, Allegheny College
                      Ideological and Attitudinal Changes in Two-Round Elections: Panel Evidence from Chile
                           Sofia Rivera Sojo, Vanderbilt University
                           Giancarlo Visconti, University of Maryland
                           Pablo Argote, University of Southern California
                      Narcopropaganda, Censorship, and Attitudes toward the State: Evidence from Cartel Music in Mexico
                           Isabel Laterzo-Tingley, University of Texas at Austin
                           Marco Alcocer, University of California - Merced
                      Discussants
                           Thiago Moreira, Louisiana State University
                           Mollie Cohen, Purdue University
                      This panel explores a diverse set of research questions related to political behavior, identities and attitudes
                      across Latin America. The papers offer new theoretical, methodological, and empirical insights into how
                      citizens navigate political and social challenges in contexts of weak state capacity, violence, and cultural
                      and political complexity. The four papers draw on innovative research designs, including survey
                      experiments, panel data, and quasi-experimental methods, to examine how political attitudes and
                      behaviors are shaped by partisan identities, complex institutions, electoral dynamics, and cultural
                      phenomena. In his paper, de la Cerda introduces the Political Identities Profiles framework, providing a
                      systematic approach to studying non-partisan political identities in Latin America, offering tools to
                      measure and validate alternative forms of identification that shape voter behavior in contexts where
                      partisanship is weak. Complementing this, Skigin, Trejo, and Mattiace examine how participatory features
                      of indigenous community policing systems in Guerrero, Mexico, foster trust and legitimacy. Their
                      findings highlight how bottom-up institutions can thrive in stratified societies where state-led reforms
                      have faltered. Turning to electoral behavior, Argote, Rivera-Sojo, and Visconti analyze Chile’s 2021
                      presidential election, showing how voters who switch candidates in runoff elections adjust their policy
                      attitudes in the short term without undergoing broader ideological shifts, underscoring the situational
                      nature of attitudinal change. Finally, Laterzo-Tingley and Alcocer explore the effects of narcopropaganda
                      and censorship in Mexico, examining how exposure to narcocorridos may undermine trust in the state
                      while state censorship efforts provoke backlash. Together, these papers advance our understanding of how
                      citizens navigate political, security, and cultural landscapes in Latin America. By integrating diverse
                      methodological approaches and contexts, the panel offers critical insights into the challenges of
                      governance, citizen engagement, and institutional legitimacy in the region.
                                                       3400
     3400            The Roads to Congress, an assessment of the 2024 elections and their political and electoral
                     implications for 2026
           Friday    Campaigns and Elections
 12:30pm-1:45pm
Bridge - Riverside   Chair
          Building       Walter Wilson, University of Texas at San Antonio
                     Discussants
                          Douglas Brattebo, University of Texas Rio Grande Valley
                          Barsky Christina, University of Vermont
                          Jeffrey Kraus, Wagner College
                          Joe Caiazzo, The JNC Group/Endicott College
                     Led by authors contributing to the “Roads to Congress 2024” book manuscript, this roundtable will
                     evaluate factors that shaped outcomes in the 2024 congressional elections, discuss their implications, and
                     look ahead to the 2026 midterms and the next edition of the Roads to Congress. Key topics for discussion
                     may include: ·       Strengthening the Roads to Congress series as a scholarly resource and pedagogical
                     tool ·      Reasons for and implications of evolving party demographic support ·        Patterns of
                     realignment in the electorate and the political implications of these trends ·    Innovations in campaign
                     strategies by candidates, parties, and groups ·      Reasons for and implications of evolving issue
                     cleavages between partisan candidates ·         Developments in and implications of recent changes in
                     election laws, including mid-decade redistricting The goals of the roundtable are to promote innovative
                     new research appearing in the ongoing “Roads to Congress” book series, highlight and build synergy
                     between emerging scholarly interpretations of contemporary electoral politics, provide opportunities for
                     scholars to network and catalyze future collaborative research on campaigns and elections, and strengthen
                     the collaboration and coherence of scholarly contributions to the next edition of the book.

     3400           CwC #5: Affective Polarization
         Friday     Conference Within A Conference
12:30pm-1:45pm
Camp - 3rd Floor    Chair
                        Matt Nelsen, University of Miami
                    Participants
                    How do you evaluate better conversations? Metric development and assessment for a multi - phase,
                          campuswide free speech program.
                          Kara Dillard, James Madison University
                    The Humanities in Action: Civic Learning Through Literature and Community Engagement
                          Lidia Radi, University of Richmond
                    Participatory and Polarized? The Link Between Partisan Animosity and Political Engagement
                          Logan Dancey, Wesleyan University
                          Christopher Chapp, St. Olaf College
                    Changes in Political Animus Amo ng College Students During the 2024 Election
                          Natalie Doerfler, UNC Charlotte
                    Growing Divided: U.S. adolescents’ perceptions of political polarization in themselves and others
                          Isobel Heck, University of Rochester
                          Nicole Park, University of Rochester
                    Discussants
                         Kelly Siegel-Stechler, Interactivity Foundation
                         Tyler Simko, University of Michigan
                    There is little doubt that the heightened interest in civic education is due at least in part to the polarized
                    state of American politics. The papers in this panel assess causes and consequences of partisan animus.
                    First, Kara Dillard assesses the impact of a campus free speech program on students ’ polarization,
                    intellectual humility, and listening skills. Next, Lidia Radi introduces students to the humanities as a tool
                    for increasing civic awareness. Logan Dancey and Christopher Chapp then wrestle with the evidence
                    showing that civic engagement can increase in the presence of affective polarization as well as in its
                    absence. Natalie Doerfler uses the 2024 presidential election to measure shifts in affective polarization.
                    And Isobel Heck shares with us how deeply divided American teenagers are.

    3400           The Politics of Resource Extraction in the Americas
         Friday    Latin American and Caribbean Politics
12:30pm-1:45pm
  Chequers - 2nd   Chair
          Floor        Nathan Edenhofer, University of California Santa Cruz
                   Participants
                   Varieties of Mining Deals in the Lithium Triangle
                        Lucas I Gonzalez, CONICET-UCA/UNSAM
                   Gold, Labor, and Crime: ASMG Workers' Organizations and Criminality in Peru
                        Omayra Peña Jimenez, Tulane University
                   Movement Leverage and Mining Capital Paralysis in Guatemala
                        Nathan Edenhofer, University of California Santa Cruz
                   Obstacles to Attaining a Social License to Operate: An Analysis of Corporate-Community Relations in
                        the Chilean Atacama Desert
                        Juliette Maasland, Tulane University
                   Green Development at the Crossroads: Lithium and the Politics of Industrial Policy in the Americas
                        Tamara Ortega-Uribe, University of California Santa Cruz
                   Discussant
                        Moises Arce, Tulane University
                   This panel brings new research on pressing questions surrounding the politics of resource extraction in the
                   Americas. Latin America is experiencing a renewed mining rush, driven by the demand for “green energy
                   transition” minerals like lithium, copper, and nickel, while the price of gold has reached historic highs
                   amid geopolitical and geo-economic tensions and uncertainty. However, unlike at the start of the
                   commodity supercycle from 2002-2013, companies seeking to extract these minerals now encounter
                   communities shaped by two decades of conflicts both against mining and over the terms of mining, while
                   debates over the benefits versus harms of extractivism have intensified. At the same time, countries are
                   increasingly engaging in the illicit and unregulated extraction of both old and new commodities, such as
                   gold and copper. With the context more relevant than ever, the papers in the panel place an emphasis on
                   power and the strategic action that communities, corporations, labor organizations, and state entities take
                   as determining the outcomes of extractive politics. These dynamics through how the variation in labor
                   organizing of artisanal gold miners across territories allows or prevents the influence of criminal
                   organizations in Peru (Peña Jimenez); how territorial conflicts and dynamics lead to negotiated,
                   unnegotiated, or aborted lithium projects in the Atacama’s Lithium Triangle of Argentina, Chile, and
                   Bolivia (González and Snyder); how the dynamics of territorial struggles led to near total national
                   paralysis of mining in Guatemala (Edenhofer); how the contradictions of company-community relations
                   create or inhibit mobilization in Chile’s lithium sector (Maasland); and how the attempts to create post-
                   neoliberal green industrial policy in the lithium sector in Chile and California expose the tensions between
                   state-led industrial policy, local opposition, neocolonial structures and geopolitics of critical minerals
                   (Ortega).

    3400          “The Victory Paradox: The Challenge of Converting Success on the Battlefield into Strategic
                  Victory in War”
         Friday   International Conflict and Security
12:30pm-1:45pm
 Commerce - 3rd   Chair
          Floor       Marc Genest, US Naval War College
                  Participants
                  “The Victory Paradox: The Challenge of Converting Success on the Battlefield into Strategic Victory in
                       the Vietnam War”
                       Cavender Sutton, Air War College
                  “The Illusion of Decision: Japan’s Inability Defeat Nationalist China”
                       Andrew Wilson, US Naval War College
                  “Israel’s War Against Hamas: The Challenge of Translating Escalation Dominance on the Battlefield to a
                       better state of Peace.”
                       Marc Genest, US Naval War College
                  China’s Gray Zone activity in the Western Pacific: Part of a Flawed Strategy
                       Craig Whiteside, US Naval War College
                  Victory as a Theoretical Paradox: The Difficulties of Understanding and Teaching Strategic Victory in
                       Great Power Competition"
                       Vanya Eftimova Bellinger, US Naval War College
                  Discussant
                       Cavender Sutton, Air War College
                  Using case studies drawn from numerous historical and contemporary wars, this panel examines the
                  challenges of converting success on the battlefield into strategic victory. While military success is
                  generally considered essential in winning a conflict, many factors make it challenging to achieve a
                  political outcome that justifies the blood and treasure expended by military operations. Panelists will
                  draw from the conflicts in Asia, Southeast Asia, the Middle East and other regions to assess the conditions
                  under which battlefield dominance is a decisive factor in achieving political objectives and when military
                  success fails to produce the desired aims of the belligerents. The following questions will be addressed.
                  Has the nature of modern warfare made military dominance more problematic? How did the political and
                  social context within which the conflict was waged impact its outcome? Where the political aims of the
                  belligerents clearly defined and realistic? Did the adversaries adequately reassess and adapt to changes on
                  the battlefield? Did international institutions and global public opinion play a meaningful role in bring the
                  war to an end?

    3400            Navigating Identity and Advocacy in US Politics
          Friday    Race and Ethnicity
12:30pm-1:45pm
Prince of Wales -   Chair
       2nd Floor        Janelle Wong, University of Maryland
                    Participants
                    Local Political Organizing, Immigrant Voting Rights, and Evidence of Resilience
                         Janelle Wong, University of Maryland
                         Megan Dias, Cornell University
                         Amena Talajawala, The George Washington University Law School
                         Lidia Rivas, CASA, Inc
                         Lydia Walther-Rodriguez, CASA, Inc
                    Complicated Loyalties: How Identities Inform Latino Attitudes about Immigration
                         Stella Rouse, Arizona State University
                         James Garand, Louisiana State University
                         Samantha Chapa, University of Notre Dame
                    The Politics of Recognition and Solidarity on the Asian American, MENA, and Jewish Left
                         Sonya Chen, Barnard College
                         Nura Sediqe, Michigan State University
                         Dara Strolovitch, Yale University
                    Criminalizing Black Womanhood: Analyzing the Reproductive Rights Landscape in the South
                         Chelsea Jones, The Brennan Center for Justice
                    White by Law, White by Choice? Racial Positioning of Iranian Americans
                         Tara Davoodi, University of Maryland
                    Discussants
                         Janelle Wong, University of Maryland
                         Kareem Crayton, The Brennan Center for Justice
                    This panel brings together a collection of studies that illuminate the interplay of identity, politics, and
                    advocacy within diverse communities across the United States. Paper topics vary from race and
                    reproductive rights, to local political organizing on immigrant rights, to immigration attitudes among
                    Latinx, to intragroup dynamics among broad identity categories, to racial identity preferences among
                    Iranian Americans. Together, the panel contributions underscore the importance of understanding identity
                    as a multifaceted and evolving construct that shapes political attitudes, political behavior, and advocacy
                    efforts. The papers are authored by scholars at various career stages, from an undergraduate student, to
                    assistant professors, to full professors. It also includes scholarship from faculty researchers as well as
                    research generated by practitioners who work in the democracy and immigrant rights spaces.

     3500           Using Star Wars and Star Trek to Examine Key Political Science Concepts in Undergraduate
                    Education
          Friday    Media and Political Communication
 2:00pm-3:15pm
Canal - 3rd Floor   Chair
                        James W. Endersby, University of Missouri
                    Participants
                    Political Leadership in the Star Trek Universe: Captains as Extension of Illustrations of the Executive
                          Branch
                          Matthew Newton, Lone Star College -- Kingwood
                    How Liberties Dies -- The Politics of the Prequel Era
                          Zachary Lang, Anderson University
                    Civil Liberties in the Star Trek Universe
                          Julie Harrelson-Stephens, Stephen F. Austin State University
                    All Who Gain Power are Afraid to Lose It: What the Star Wars Saga Can Teach Us about the Political
                          World Today
                          Chapman Rackaway, Radford University
                    Understanding Race and Ethnicity: Star Trek’s Examination of Interspecies and Intraspecies Interactions
                          as a Mirror of American Politics
                          Steven Galatas, Stephen F. Austin State University
                          Kevin Anderson, Eastern Illinois University
                    Discussant
                         Michael Smith, Emporia State University
                    This panel provides an overview of how political science concepts may be taught through the lens of two
                    of the best known science fiction franchises: Star Trek and Star Wars. Various papers address these
                    concepts using the entire cannon of movies and television series as example and illustrations of civil
                    rights, race and ethnic relations, civil liberties, regime change, democratization, executive power, and
                    separation of powers. The various papers attempt to thematically explore political science as presented in
                    American government and comparative politics courses. Presenters include individuals from regional
                    state universities, private universities, community colleges, and research universities.
                                                       3500
     3500            Rethinking Revolutionary Cuba: Politics, Diaspora, and Reform
          Friday     Latin American and Caribbean Politics
  2:00pm-3:15pm
   Chequers - 2nd    Chair
           Floor         Christopher M. Brown, Georgia Southern University
                     Participants
                     Private Sector Corruption and Its Influence on Public Sector Corruption in Cuba
                          Ivana Casuso, Georgia Southern University
                          Christopher M. Brown, Georgia Southern University
                     Legacy of the Cuban Diaspora on Contemporary US Foreign Policy: How Refugees of the Cuban
                          Revolution Slow Diplomatic Mending
                          Spencer Garland, Georgia Southern University
                          Christopher M. Brown, Georgia Southern University
                     Discussant
                          Georgina Chami, University of the West Indies
                     This panel explores the multifaceted legacies of revolutionary Cuba through historical, legal, and
                     transnational lenses. Anchored in the Cold War moment, one paper revisits the Bay of Pigs invasion to
                     interrogate John F. Kennedy’s strategic calculus and its implications for U.S.–Cuban relations. Another
                     examines the entanglement of public and private corruption in Cuba, revealing how informal networks
                     and state structures co-evolved under communism. A third contribution traces the enduring influence of
                     the Cuban diaspora on contemporary U.S. foreign policy, highlighting how exile communities have
                     shaped legislative agendas and diplomatic postures. Finally, a legal-historical analysis of gender reforms
                     in revolutionary Cuba assesses both the progressive aspirations and persistent limitations of state-led
                     transformation. Together, these papers present a nuanced portrait of Cuba, not as a static revolutionary
                     symbol, but as a dynamic site of contestation, where ideology, governance, and identity intersect across
                     borders and over decades. By bridging domestic and international perspectives, the panel invites critical
                     reflection on Cuba’s evolving role in global politics and the complex interplay between reform, resistance,
                     and remembrance.

     3500           Building university and community ties: Exploring different ways to have impact
          Friday    Public Administration
 2:00pm-3:15pm
Eglinton Winton -   Chair
       2nd Floor        Bridget Kathleen Gallagher, Texas Tech University
                    Participants
                    From outreach to impact: Evaluating university engagement strategies
                         Leigh Hersey, University of Louisiana Monroe
                         Bridget Kathleen Gallagher, Texas Tech University
                    From strengths to struggles: Rural community engagement in West Texas
                         Jason Headrick, Texas Tech University
                         Erica Irlbeck, Texas Tech University
                    Defending the defenders: How universities strengthen communities through interdisciplinary public
                         defense training
                         Leigh Hersey, University of Louisiana Monroe
                         Anissa Horne, University of Louisiana Monroe
                         Courtney Harris, University of Louisiana Monroe
                    Great libraries build communities: How are contemporary libraries collaborating community
                         development?
                         Bridget Kathleen Gallagher, Texas Tech University
                         Leigh Hersey, University of Louisiana Monroe
                         Divya Janardhan, Texas Tech University
                         Jason Headrick, Texas Tech University
                         Daniel Scheller, Texas Tech University
                    Academic Utilization and Issue Novelty
                         Connor William Dye, University of Texas at San Antonio
                    Discussant
                         Leigh Hersey, University of Louisiana Monroe
                    Higher education organizations (HEOs) are being called to become more engaged with their communities.
                    Kerr (2001), in some of the most well-known literature in this area, highlights the role of universities
                    within society arguing that place and space are central to understanding how HEOs integrate themselves
                    within their societies and communities. From the initial land grant universities to the more recent
                    Carnegie Community Engagement Elective Classification, different models of engagement and impact
                    have been put forth. This panel will explore these models and practices of engagement. The first paper
                    examines the missions of land grant universities, urban-serving universities, extension programs, and
                    engaged scholarship, identifying overlaps and gaps. The next paper focuses on rural community research
                    conducted in West Texas (the Panhandle and South Plains regions), including the presence of third spaces
                    and the strengths and challenges of these communities. The third paper presents how a regional university
                    has implemented a statewide training program for public defenders. The final paper demonstrates how
                    partnering public libraries can fill the engagement gaps, particularly in rural communities. Attendees will
                    be able to take this knowledge back to their own universities and develop and enhance their own projects
                    to engage with their communities.

     3600            Race, Capital and Politics in Global Cities
           Friday    Comparative and International Political Economy
  3:30pm-4:45pm
Parish - 3rd Floor   Chair
                         Alfonso Gonzales, UC Riverside
                     Participants
                     The Impact of Neoliberal Restructuring on Latino/a/x Workers in the Logistics and Warehousing Sector
                          of the Inland Empire
                          Pedro Freire, University of California, Riverside
                     Latino Las Vegas: Labor, Law of Accumulation and the Evermore Brutal Social Relations of Capital
                          Alfonso Gonzales, UC Riverside
                     Race, Class, and the Marxist Critique of the State
                          DeVin Johnson, UCR
                     Neo-Liberal Capitalism and its Impact on Coffee Market in Tanzania and Colombia
                          Murrell Brooks, Soka University of America
                     Discussants
                          Alfonso Gonzales, UC Riverside
                          Pedro Freire, University of California, Riverside
                     This panel will feature a generation of emerging and established scholars analyzing the relationship
                     between race and the political economy in urban spaces. The scholars are particularly focused on
                     comparative analyses of urban centers such as New Orleans and Cartagena, Colombia, the Inland Empire
                     of California, as well as Philadelphia and São Paulo, Brazil. These cities exemplify how racialized
                     identities are simultaneously commodified and valorized within industry, while facing spatial policing and
                     criminalization. Scholars in this panel are interested in the ways that race, social polarization,
                     commodification, labor, and criminalization play out in the politics of comparative global cities.

   3700           The City in Speech and Speech in the City
        Friday    Political Theory
5:00pm-6:15pm
 Magazine - 3rd   Chair
         Floor        Terry Hughston, University of Alabama
                  Participants
                  Minding One’s Own Business: Socratic Political Philosophy and the City
                        Samuel Hage, Tulane University
                  Setting the City in Motion: Politics and Natural Science in Plato’s Timaeus
                        Evan Coulter, University of Texas, Austin
                  The City in Song: Origins, Music, and Political Life in Rousseau
                        Nicholas Allmaier, Clemson University
                  Socrates’ Dream and the Possibility of Platonic Political Philosophy in the Crito
                        Matthew Oberrieder, Rogers State University
                  Discussant
                       daniel levine, University of Alabama
                  According to the classical tradition, and articulated most famously by Aristotle, human beings are by
                  nature both rational and political animals. As Aristotle goes on to say, politics is prior by nature to the
                  human being; humanity as such, that is, is impossible without the rational faculty developed by communal
                  living. Speech (logos) comes to sight as logically prior but ontologically posterior to political life: politics
                  is the subject of speech by political philosophers, who debate about justice and the advantageous, but
                  politics also presupposes the ability to speak and reason about these same subjects. This panel explores
                  speech as both the sine qua non of the practice of politics, and also the means by which theorists treat the
                  subject of political life.

       3700            Author Meets Critics: Saladin Ambar's Murder on the Mississippi: The Shocking Crimes That
                       Shaped Abraham Lincoln
             Friday    Presidential and Executive Politics
    5:00pm-6:15pm
Steering - Riverside   Chair
            Building       Justin Vaughn, Coastal Carolina University
                       Discussants
                            Saladin Ambar, Rutgers University
                            Jamie Carson, University of Georgia
                            Richard M. Yon, United States Military Academy
                       This panel will feature a robust discussion of Saladin Ambar's recent book, Murder on the Mississippi:
                       The Shocking Crimes that Shaped Abraham Lincoln (Diversion). Amid the string of murders on the
                       American frontier, Lincoln faced the loss of his first love—and a descent into suicidal despair. Yet from
                       this darkness, he emerged with a renewed purpose, one that would define his leadership in the fight for
                       democracy, human freedom, and the rule of law. In Murder on the Mississippi, Ambar unearths the
                       horrors that shaped a young Abraham Lincoln’s worldview, pushing him to find his political voice in one
                       of the earliest and most pivotal speeches of his career. Confronted by lawlessness, racial terror, and his
                       own inner demons, Lincoln’s battle was political and deeply personal. From the flames of mob violence
                       rose a young Lincoln, forged in fire and soon to contend with a nation at war with itself.

     4100            Reforming Oversight in an Era of Executive Supremacy
         Saturday    Presidential and Executive Politics
  8:00am-9:15am
Churchill B2 - 2nd   Chair
            Floor        Jesse Crosson, Purdue University
                     Participants
                     Building Congressional Processing to Respond to Loper-Bright
                           Soren Dayton, The Foundation for American Innovation
                     Civil Service Reform: Recent Theoretical and Empirical Work
                           Daniel Gibbs, Virginia Tech
                     Making Oversight Great Again: Reforming Congressional Investigations in the Age of Trump
                           Claire Leavitt, Smith College
                     Discussant
                          Henry B. Sirgo, McNeese State University
                     This panel examines the institutional challenges and opportunities facing executive-congressional
                     relations in an era of rapid legal and political changes. One paper explores how the demise of the Chevron
                     doctrine creates a need for Congress to develop procedural mechanisms that activate legislative decision-
                     making based on expert agency findings. Another critically surveys emerging literature on civil service
                     reform, focusing on the sometimes counterintuitive implications of proposals like Schedule F for
                     bureaucratic accountability and politicization. The final paper analyzes how the Trump administration
                     exposed the fragility of congressional oversight, and offers historical and practical reforms to strengthen
                     investigative capacity in the face of executive resistance. Together, these papers offer a roadmap for
                     rebalance congressional and executive power, focusing on expertise, procedural muscle, and institutional
                     resilience in the post-Chevron era.

                                                        4100
   4100           Politics, Religion, and the Challenges of Commercial Society in Modern Political Thought
      Saturday    Political Theory
8:00am-9:15am
 Magazine - 3rd   Chair
         Floor        Lee Ward, Baylor University
                  Participants
                  A Covenant Renewed: Hobbesian Apocalypse and the End of Oligarchic Time
                       Boleslaw Kabala, Tarleton State University
                  Reconciling Theological Norms with Locke’s Political Thought
                       Jacob Rodriguez, Baylor University
                  John Locke, Sacraments, and Politics
                       Kevin Matti Kearns, Texas A&M University-Corpus Christi
                  On Becoming "Altogether Unwarlike": Comparing the Scots on the Status of Martial Virtues in a
                       Commercial Society.
                       Will Jordan, Mercer University
                  Commerce, Modern Statecraft, and the Wealth of Moral Sentiments: Montesquieu and Adam Smith
                       Constantine Vassiliou, University of Colorado, Colorado Springs
                  Discussants
                       Joey Barretta, Washington & Lee University
                       James Stoner, Louisiana State University
                  In the modern period, political thinkers in England, Scotland, and France grappled with two major
                  challenges: (1) the relation between politics and religion, and (2) the potential morally and politically
                  corrosive effects of commerce. This panel includes a number of papers that examine these important
                  issues from several different perspectives. One group of papers explores the relation between politics and
                  religion in Thomas Hobbes and John Locke. Boleslaw Kabala argues that while Hobbes intended his
                  political theory to neutralize potentially seditious religious doctrines, he also employed apocalyptic
                  rhetoric that overlapped with his religious opponents and indirectly reinforced underappreciated
                  democratic possibilities in Hobbes’ work. Jacob Rodriguez and Kevin Kearns focus on Locke. Rodriguez
                  engages with the perennial debate among Locke scholars as to whether Locke is a secular or religious
                  thinker. Rodriguez proposes a third way according to which Locke’s Christian and liberal commitments
                  are necessarily interrelated in the epistemic properties of the individual self. Kearns examines the role of
                  the Christian sacraments in Locke’s treatment of theology, especially in his often-neglected Paraphrase
                  and Notes on the Epistles of St. Paul and argues that there are serious political implications in Locke’s
                  interpretation of the sacraments. A second pair of papers explores Montesquieu and the Scottish
                  Enlightenment response to the challenges of the emerging commercial society. Will Jordan focusses on
                  the concern Scottish thinkers such as David Hume, Adam Ferguson and Adam Smith felt about
                  commercial society’s inability to sustain military readiness and martial virtues. Jordan argues that each of
                  these important thinkers offered a unique response to the problem of martial virtue that continues to
                  influence debate in commercial republics today. Constantine Vassiliou compares and contrasts
                  Montesquieu and Adam Smith’s proposals for a new civic statecraft in response to the potentially
                  corrosive effects of commerce on liberal governments and society. Vassiliou argues that Smith and
                  Montesquieu sought to encourage future statesmen to harmonize their civic projects to accommodate local
                  prejudices, customs and institutions. In total, this panel offers an in-depth and yet also comprehensive
                  treatment of some of the most important topics in modern political thought

     4200           Author Meets Critics Roundtable: "Religious Liberty: A Conservative Primer" by John Wilsey
      Saturday      Religion and Politics
9:30am-10:45am
Cambridge - 2nd     Discussants
          Floor          Glenn A Moots, Northwood University
                         Michael Federici, Middle Tennessee State University
                         James Patterson, University of Tennessee Knoxville
                         Michael Lucchese, Liberty Fund
                         John Wilsey, Southern Baptist Theological Seminary
                    This “Author Meets Critics” roundtable will discuss John Wilsey's book “Religious Liberty: A
                    Conservative Primer" (Eerdmans). Wilsey advances what he calls "aspirational conservatism" to revive
                    conservatism in an age of postliberalism, populism, and MAGA. Of particular interest to Wilsey is the
                    phenomena of religious postliberalism (the revival of integralism or magisterial Protestantism) and its
                    consequences for liberalism. The panel will discuss the survival of traditional conservatism in an age of
                    populism and MAGA and the religious dimensions of liberalism and postliberalism. Roundtable
                    Participants: James Patterson (University of Tennessee Knoxville), Michael Federici (Middle Tennessee
                    State University), Michael Lucchese (Hudson Institute/Hillsdale/Liberty Fund), Glenn Moots (Northwood
                    University)

     4200            Reforming Courts
         Saturday    Judicial Politics
 9:30am-10:45am
Churchill B2 - 2nd   Chair
            Floor        Jesse Crosson, Purdue University
                     Participants
                     Embracing Institutional Change
                           Eileen Braman, Indiana University
                     Mitigating Perceptions of Political Decision Making: A Modest Supreme Court Proposal
                           Sara Benesh, University of Wisconsin - Milwaukee
                     The Supreme Court and Sortition: An Ancient Solution for a Modern Problem
                           James Wallner, R Street Institute & Clemson University
                     Israeli Judicial Reforms: A Necessary Democratic Correction or an Autocratic Shift?
                           Jonathan Snow, Roanoke College
                     Discussant
                          Logan Strother, Purdue University
                     With confidence in American institutions at historic lows and policymaking bodies seemingly incapable
                     of addressing an ever-growing list of societal challenges, the idea of institutional reform has grown in
                     popularity across the U.S. This panel will consider potential statutory reforms to the federal judiciary.
                     This includes analysis of the proper scope of judicial authority (as established by Congress under Article
                     III of the Constitution), the politics of authorization of judicial seats (which has key implications for the
                     efficient administration of justice and judicial capacity), and the way Supreme Court justices are selected.

    4200            Disrupting Political Science: Black Women Reimagining the Discipline
        Saturday    Race and Ethnicity

                    A panel of Black female political science professors and students will discuss their experiences as

                    chapters in the Disrupting Political Science: Black Women Reimagining the Discipline book, edited by

                                                        4400
     4400            CwC #13: Faculty Panel: Ancients vs. Moderns
         Saturday    Conference Within A Conference
 12:30pm-1:45pm
Churchill B2 - 2nd   Chair
            Floor        Ronna Burger, Tulane University
                     Participants
                     Herodotus, Aeschylus, and Sophocles on the Ancient Political Project
                          Frank Pagano, St. John's College, Annapolis
                     Aristotle ’ s Political Dialogue
                          Rachel Mackey, Hudson Institute
                     Montesquieu ’ s Persian Letters: Enlightenment and Despotism
                          Janet Dougherty, St. John's College, Annapolis
                     Machiavelli ’ s Knives
                          Michael Hoffapuir, UATX
                     The capstone panel of our CWC puts in tension ancient and modern political philosophy. Dr. Frank
                     Pagano (St. John ’ s College, Annapolis) considers antiquity as a project articulated by Herdotus,
                     Sophocles, and Aeschylus. Dr. Rachel Mackey (Hudson Institute) articulates Aristotle ’ s understanding
                     of the fundamental conditions for political life and asks whether the classical notion of politics still exists,
                     or indeed can exist, in the modern age. Dr. J. Michael Hoffpauir (University of Austin) explores in
                     Machiavelli ’ s mentions of “ knives ” his modern conceptions of fortune, god, cruelty, and the divine.
                     Janet Dougherty (St. John ’ s College, Annapolis) concludes with a book that bridges ancient and modern
                     thought, Montesquieu ’ s Persian Letters . She inquire s about the relationship between the modern
                     scientific method and ancient philosophy ’ s quest for self - knowledge, as well as between despotism and
                     enlightened government. Taken together, these presentations aim to present ancient and modern
                     conceptions of politics and philosophy and ask what students of politics can learn by putting them in
                     dialogue with one another. Panel members will respond to one another.

     4500            CwC #13: Student Panel: On the Ancients
         Saturday    Conference Within A Conference
  2:00pm-3:15pm
Churchill B2 - 2nd   Chair
            Floor        Frank Pagano, St. John's College, Annapolis
                     Participants
                     The Necessity of Thymos for Philosophic Inquiry
                          Craig Koch, Tulane University
                     The Implications of Socratic Philosophy for a Secular Society: Justice and the Holy in Plato's Euthyphro
                          Dylan Jackson, St. John's College, Annapolis
                     Xenophon ’ s Playful Posse: Lightheartedness in Portrayals of Socrates
                          Emma Bernstein MacFarlane, Baylor University
                     Aristotle on Thymos and Moral Education
                          Maria Goyette, Baylor University
                     Discussant
                          Rachel Mackey, Hudson Institute
                     This graduate student panel aims to understand philosophy as a way of life: particularly, the proper
                     ordering of soul and community that the ancients thought was a necessary precondition for philosophy.
                     Craig Koch (Tulane University) investigates “ the spirited relationship to truth without which the
                     philosophers ’ erotic pursuit of truth would not be possible. ” Dylan Jackson (St. John ’ s College,
                     Annapolis) reads the Euthyphro for lessons relevant to a modern secular nation. Emma Bernstien
                     MacFarlane asks whether “ lightheartedness [is] something Xenophon understands to be a necessary part
                     of the good life, ” and why Socrates countenances the presence of lighthearted men in his company. Maria
                     Goyette (Baylor University, fellow at Cleveland State University) takes up how Aristotle ’ s moral
                     education aims to refine the quality of spiritedness, so important to politics and a well - ordered soul.
                     These presentations will suggest how Socrates, Plato, Xenophon, and Aristotle shape the education of the
                     philosopher while articulating the shocking claim that philosophy is essential for politics.

      4500            Seeking Research Funding from Foundations: Grant and Fellowship Opportunities for Political
                      Scientists
          Saturday    Program Chair's Panels
   2:00pm-3:15pm
Jackson - 3rd Floor   Chair
                          James A Wilson, Russell Sage Foundation
                      Participant
                      Seeking Research Funding from Foundations: Grant and Fellowship Opportunities for Political Scientists
                           James A Wilson, Russell Sage Foundation
                      Discussants
                           Andra Gillespie, Emory University
                           Lee Walker, National Science Foundation
                           Jan Leighley, National Science Foundation
                      Seeking research funding is an increasingly competitive process and preparing a successful application
                      can be a challenging and time-consuming exercise. This session will provide a practical overview of
                      applying for research support in political science from two high-profile funders of social, political and
                      policy research. The panel includes (1) program staff from from the National Science Foundation and the
                      Russell Sage Foundation, and (2) academic scholars who have applied for and received funding as well as
                      served as reviewers for the process. Funder panelists will discuss their funding priorities, the grant writing
                      and review process and the basics of what makes a good grant proposal, processes for developing ideas
                      for funding and budgeting, and resources that specify what to include in research proposals. With
                      extensive funding experience and evaluating proposals as part of the grant-making process, funder
                      panelists will discuss how to develop and write a sound grant application from the perspective of a
                      funding organization. Scholar panelists will discuss and provide insights about the application and review
                      process from the grant-seeking side. A key focus will be on tips for good grant-writing – the specifics of
                      what often makes for a good grant proposal and the mistakes that grant-seekers should avoid in drafting
                      an application.
                                                     4500
   4500           Reconsidering Plato’s Caves: The Liberating Sting of Cultural Diversity
      Saturday    Political Theory
2:00pm-3:15pm
 Magazine - 3rd   Chair
         Floor        Ann Ward, Baylor University
                  Discussants
                       Rebecca LeMoine, Florida Atlantic University
                       Robert Ballingall, University of Maine
                       Alex Orwin, Louisiana State University
                       Mary Townsend, St. john's University
                       Jacob Rodriguez, Baylor University
                  Given the rise of illiberalism, nativism, and authoritarianism in our politics today, bringing with it the
                  attack on the “woke” concepts of diversity, equity and inclusion and promises of the detention and mass
                  deportations of immigrants, it is all the more imperative that we reflect on the impact of Plato’s Caves:
                  The Liberating Sting of Cultural Diversity (Oxford University Press, 2020) by Rebecca LeMoine. In
                  Plato’s Caves, LeMoine argues, contrary to the popular and scholarly narrative, that Plato was a friend of
                  cultural diversity. Through a careful reading of four Platonic dialogues, the Republic, Menexenus, Laws,
                  and Phaedrus, LeMoine shows that far from exhibiting hostility toward foreigners, they reveal that
                  foreigners paly a role similar to that of Socrates: the role of gadfly. Like encounters with Socrates,
                  interactions with foreigners can expose contradictions in the authoritative opinions that undergird one’s
                  political community and thus encourage a more self-reflective citizenship. They can do this, according to
                  LeMoine, because every citizen lives in a polity that shares the basic features of the cave, Plato’s famous
                  allegory in the Republic. This means that when we interact with someone raised in a different “cave,” we
                  are faced with the possibility of another way of life and hence with the occasion to reflect on our own
                  conception of truth, beauty, and goodness. Foreigners, in other words, help us to see our cave as a cave,
                  hence showing us that we live in a world of cave-like polities from which we can nonetheless be freed.
                  LeMoine also argues that studying Plato’s views on foreigner’s gives deeper insight into his attitudes
                  toward democracy, and concludes with reflections on how Plato’s portrayal of foreigners as gadflies can
                  speak to our contemporary debate on cultural diversity. According to LeMoine, Plato shows how the
                  “liberating sting” of cross-cultural engagement, although initially painful, can help citizens cultivate a
                  Socratic knowledge of ignorance and hence the intellectual humility that is essential to the preservation of
                  democracy. Plato, LeMoine argues, shows that democracy’s downfall into tyranny happens when citizens
                  develop intellectual hubris that rejects education and the exposure to cultural diversity that it brings.
                  Participants: Rebecca LeMoine, Florida Atlantic University, rlemoine@fau.edu Robert Ballingall,
                  University of Maine, Robert.ballingall@maine.edu Enji Lin, Baylor University, angela_lin1@baylor.edu
                  Alex Orwin, Louisiana State University, aorwin1@lsu.edu Mary Townsend, St. John’s University,
                  townsenm@stjohns.edu Ann Ward, Baylor University, ann_ward@baylor.edu

     4600            The Political Economy of Influence: Money, Expertise, and Geography in Elections
         Saturday    Campaigns and Elections
  3:30pm-4:45pm
Bridge - Riverside   Participants
          Building   Investing in Political Expertise: The Remarkable Scale of Corporate Policy Teams
                           Andrew Hall, Stanford
                     Do interest group endorsements matter?
                           Alex Fouirnaies, University of Chicago
                     Political Geography and Distributive Politics in the 21st Century
                           Chris Berry, University of Chicago
                           Anthony Fowler, University of Chicago
                     Money and Motivations: When Do Campaign Contributions Create Polarization?
                           Peter Buisseret, Harvard University
                           Pablo Montagnes, Emory University
                           Richard Van Weelden, University of Pittsburgh
                     This panel examines how organizations, money, and geography shape American politics. Hall and Sun
                     document the rise of corporate policy teams, showing that firms increasingly invest more in internal
                     political expertise than in traditional lobbying. Berry and Fowler trace the shifting geography of economic
                     productivity and partisan alignment, highlighting how the new political economy of place reshapes
                     distributive politics. Fouirnaies shows how interest group endorsements tilt primary elections,
                     underscoring how organized groups channel support to favored candidates. Finally, Buisseret presents a
                     formal model of elections in which the incentives of campaigns to raise money from small donors leads to
                     polarized platforms. Together, these papers reveal the diverse ways in which money, expertise,
                     geography, and group support shape contemporary American politics, offering new perspectives on the
                     anatomy of influence beyond the ballot box.

      4600           CwC #12: Validation and LLMs in Political Science
         Saturday    Conference Within A Conference
  3:30pm-4:45pm
Churchill C1 - 2nd   Discussants
            Floor         Ethan C Busby, Brigham Young University
                          Lisa Argyle, Purdue University
                          Joshua Gubler, Brigham Young University
                     This roundtable discussion follows up on a related working group at the 2025 APSA meeting in
                     Vancouver, CA. It is intended as a step towards developing a set of validation practices and standards for
                     working with generative AI tools in political science. Work using generative AI tools in political science
                     has dramatically increased, but researchers still lack a set of best practices to validate and justify the use
                     of generative AI tools in their many applications. This leaves scholars, reviewers, funders, and the
                     discipline at large unsure how to evaluate the quality of research using generative AI in political science.
                     We intend for the scope of this conversation to include best practices and evaluation standards related, but
                     not limited, to: extent and disclosure of AI use in the research process, transparency and replicability,
                     metrics and benchmarks for task - specific performance, and model selection

   4700          Violence, repression and democratic contestation in Latin America
      Saturday   Latin American and Caribbean Politics
5:00pm-6:15pm
Commerce - 3rd   Chair
         Floor       Kaitlyn Chriswell, University of Miami
                 Participants
                 Claiming the State Through Violence: Lynching and State Engagement in Mexico
                       Hannah Baron, Xavier University of Louisiana
                 Contesting crime: Non-state strategies of community resistance
                       Kaitlyn Chriswell, University of Miami
                 Stigmatizing Defiance: Citizen Attitudes Toward Violence Against Women and Indigenous Activists in
                       Post-Accord Colombia
                       Isabel Guiza-Gomez, Tulane University
                 Law, Violence, and Emergency in Jamaica: Early Post-Independence Declarations of Emergency (1974-
                       1977)
                       Jermaine Young, HOWARD UNIVERSITY
                 Political Violence as a Vocation: How Politicians Shape Citizens’ Demand for Repression
                       Natán Skigin, University of Georgia
                 Discussant
                      Hannah Baron, Xavier University of Louisiana
                 How do citizens and political actors navigate violence, and how do their strategies shape democracy and
                 state authority? This panel brings together research on Colombia, Mexico, and Brazil to examine the
                 politics between victims, movements, and politicians in contexts of conflict and insecurity. In Colombia,
                 unarmed movements mobilize for land redistribution even amid armed conflict to achieve redistributive
                 reform through peace processes. In some parts of Mexico, victims and local communities leverage
                 violence through extralegal practice to engage with or pressure state institutions, while in others, they
                 organize collectively but deliberately avoid state institutions. In Brazil, politicians strategically deploy
                 repression-based appeals that influence public support for police violence and shape attitudes toward
                 police reforms. Taken together, these studies show how citizens and elites negotiate the terms of violence
                 and order, with consequences for redistribution, state accountability, and democratic governance in Latin
                 America.
